Summary

Fixed-energy Response-function Analysis with Multiple eﬃciency (FRAM) is a software code designed
primarily for plutonium and uranium isotopic analysis. It is widely used in both the domestic and
international safeguards community. FRAM can quickly and accurately determine the isotopic
compositions of plutonium, uranium, and mixed oxides (MOX) when measuring with a high-purity
germanium (HPGe), cadmium zinc telluride (CZT), or lanthanum bromide (LaBr3) detector. The
capabilities of FRAM have been enhanced to analyze the data of the pixelated CZT detector (made by
H3D) and to measure the mass of plutonium, uranium, and MOX. Both the isotopic composition and
mass of the item can be quickly determined with one measurement using a gamma detector with FRAM
v.7.1.

Task 1: Overview

A. INTRODUCTION

The Fixed-Energy Response-Function Analysis with Multiple Eﬃciency (FRAM) software was developed
by Los Alamos National Laboratory and is still reﬁned continuously. It was ﬁrst developed in the mid-
1980s, running on a MicroVAX computer with the VMS operating system. The ﬁrst version of the FRAM
code running on PC/Windows 3.1, called PC/FRAM, was released in 1994. This version could analyze
plutonium spectra using gamma ray peaks above 120 keV.

Released in 1997, FRAM version 2 added limited capability to analyze uranium spectra measured at a
distance greater than 15 cm using peaks above 120 keV. It was the ﬁrst commercially available version
which ran on Windows 3.1 and 95 and was licensed to Ortec.

FRAM version 3 was released in 1999 as a 32-bit code, running on Windows 95, 98, and NT. It was
licensed to Ortec and Canberra.

FRAM version 4 was released late 2001 and early 2002 and was last updated with some bug ﬁxes in
October 2002. It added the capability to analyze the 100-keV X-ray region of both plutonium and
uranium spectra. It also corrected for the uranium peak summing issue which let it analyze uranium data
at all distances. It ran on Windows 95, 98, NT, 2000, and XP. It was licensed to Ortec and Canberra.

FRAM version 5.1 was released in 2011 with a completely diﬀerent user interface and the capability to
run in the command line mode. It ran on all 32-bit and 64-bit Windows platforms from Windows 95 to
Windows 10. It was licensed to Ortec and Canberra. FRAM v.5.2 was released in 2013 with some minor
upgrades from v.5.1 and was also licensed to Ortec and Canberra.

FRAM version 6.1 was released in 2020. Its major upgrade was the added capability to analyze the LaBr3
and CZT spectra. It ran on Windows XP, 7, 8, and 10 [1]. It was licensed to Ortec and Canberra.

We have upgraded FRAM and are releasing it as FRAM version 7.1. One major upgrade of FRAM v.7.1 is
its capability to analyze the uranium/plutonium/MOX spectra measured by the pixelated CZT detector
made by H3D. Another improvement, perhaps the most important one, is its capability to measure the
mass of plutonium/uranium/MOX. With this new capability, both the isotopic composition and mass of

the nuclear item can be quickly determined with a single measurement using a gamma detector. It has
not been extensively tested, but it should be able to run on Windows 7, 8, 10, 11, and on most Windows
XP operating systems.

B. IMPROVEMENTS

1. Analyzing H3D CZT data

a. Fitting the peaks of H3D CZT spectra

The company H3D produces a new type of large, pixelated CZT detector with FWHM better than 1% at
662 keV. However, the peaks of the H3D CZT detector have a large high-energy tail. FRAM v.6.1 code can
ﬁt the low-energy tail of a peak but is not equipped to ﬁt a high-energy tail.

FRAM was upgraded to include the capability to ﬁt the high-energy tail of a peak in addition to the low-
energy tail. Figure 1 shows the examples of the least-square ﬁts of the M400’s peaks by FRAM v.6.1 and
the upgraded FRAM v.7.1.

FRAM v.6.1 fails to ﬁt the high-energy tails of the peaks, while FRAM v.7.1 can ﬁt the high-energy tails. In
FRAM v.6.1, the ﬁts of the peaks gave peak area results that are smaller than the actual peak areas. The
underestimation of a peak area depended on the size of its high-energy tail: the larger the tail, the larger
the underestimation. Additionally, the magnitude of a tail is generally a function of energy; higher-
energy peaks are aﬀected by larger tails and therefore result in more severe underestimation.

Even though FRAM 6.1 cannot ﬁt the high-energy tail of a peak, the uranium analysis employing the
high-energy regions can still analyze the data and achieve reasonable results. The reason for the
successful analysis with FRAM v.6.1 is that the uranium peaks are uncrowded, and most of the intense
peaks, when ﬁtted without the high-energy tail, will underestimate the peak areas proportionally as a
function of peak energies. For this gradual change of peak area, FRAM can still analyze the data and
produce satisfactory results.

This reasoning does not work with plutonium data. The plutonium peaks are crowded, and many peaks
are clustered together. When ﬁtting such peaks using a peak shape without the high-energy tail, some
peaks will be underestimated while others will be overestimated depending on the peak magnitudes and
locations within the cluster. Therefore, the upgraded FRAM with the capability to ﬁt the high-energy tail
is required to analyze plutonium data.

b. Diﬃculties in ﬁtting the H3D CZT data

Unlike the Gaussian peak shape, which quickly drops to the baseline, the H3D CZT peak shape with large
low- and high-energy tails drops slowly to the baseline. This eﬀectively widens the ﬁt region and make it
diﬃcult to set the background regions for a proper ﬁt. Figure 2 shows diﬀerent peak and background
regions about the 1001-keV peak, the least-square ﬁts of the 1001-keV peak, and the resulting areas
from the ﬁts.

All the ﬁts of the same 1001-keV peak with three diﬀerent sets of background regions initially appear to
be good but give signiﬁcantly diﬀerent results. A solution to that issue is to use diﬀerent branching ratios
for diﬀerent sets of background regions such that the ratio of area to branching ratio remains constant.
This means that the peak branching ratios employed in the H3D CZT spectrum analysis are incorrect and
diﬀerent from the ones of the HPGe spectrum analysis.

c. Measurements and analysis

We measured many plutonium and uranium sources. The plutonium items consisted of 10 items with 3–
26% 240Pu and masses from 0.4 to 600 g. The uranium items consisted of 15 items with enrichments
ranging from 0.2 to 93% and masses from 4 g to 1000 g. Some items were measured only once, while
others were measured by two or more conﬁgurations and distances. The M400 acquires and saves the
data in list mode format. Each data set spanned about 4 hours or longer. Each data set was then divided
into multiple spectra of about 30 minutes real time each. The base ﬁlter for a plutonium item is 0.8–1.6
mm cadmium and the base ﬁlter for a uranium item is none. Several items were measured with an
additional 9 mm iron or 0.8 mm lead ﬁlter. The spectra with iron ﬁlter were analyzed by all parameter
sets, but the ones with lead ﬁlter were analyzed with only the 180–800-keV plutonium parameter set.

We created new parameter sets for analyzing the M400 data. For plutonium, the analysis was separated
into weapon grade (WG) with 240Pu ~< 10% and reactor grade (RG) with 240Pu ~> 10%. The analysis was
further separated into middle (120-460-keV) and high (180-800-keV) energy regions. The parameter sets
for plutonium analysis are CZT-H3D_PuWG_120-460, CZT-H3D_PuRG_120-460, CZT-H3D_PuWG_180-
800, and CZT-H3D_PuRG_180-800. (The parameter set name shows three groups separated by the
underscores: the ﬁrst group is the detector type, the second group is the material type, and the third
group is the analysis energy range.) The parameter sets for low enriched uranium (LEU) and high
enriched uranium (HEU) are CZT-H3D_ULEU_120-1010 and CZT-H3D_UHEU_120-1010, respectively.

The data were analyzed by FRAM v.7.1 using the physical eﬃciency model. The FRAM results of
plutonium speciﬁc power, plutonium eﬀective Pu-240, and uranium enrichment were analyzed by the
analysis of variance (ANOVA) to estimate the repeatability standard deviation and the standard deviation
of the item-speciﬁc bias in each data set.

ANOVA: The FRAM measurement error model can be expressed as

𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆 is the item-speciﬁc systematic uncertainty, and 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 is the random uncertainty.

The error variance can be partitioned into within-group variance and between-group variance [2].

Table 1 shows the ANOVA results of the uranium and plutonium data. For each analysis, two diﬀerent
sets of results are shown: the random uncertainty R and the item-speciﬁc systematic uncertainty 𝛿𝛿𝑆𝑆.

The estimate of 𝛿𝛿R represents a global random or statistical uncertainty for FRAM, drawn from the speciﬁc
data sets used in the analysis. The random uncertainty from a measurement can be larger or smaller than
this, depending on the statistics of the data when compared with those represented in the data sets.

The estimate of 𝛿𝛿𝑆𝑆 represents the item-speciﬁc systematic uncertainty for an isotopic analysis of a
spectrum measured with a detector system like those used to acquire the data sets in this work and good
measurement techniques. A detector with better eﬃciency and/or resolution/peak shape than the one
used in this work can give better systematic uncertainty for the same parameter set.

2. MASS MEASUREMENT

a. Mass measurement principle

FRAM determines the isotopic composition of special nuclear materials (SNM) by ﬁrst ﬁtting the peaks to
obtain the peak areas. From the peak areas, it calculates the relative eﬃciency (by ﬁtting the eﬃciency
curve), then the isotopic ratios of the isotopes, and then ﬁnally the isotopic fractions of the isotopes. The
physical eﬃciency curve of FRAM v.6.1 and earlier includes a built-in, generic detector eﬃciency curve.
The FRAM v.7.1 code includes the option to enter and use an actual detector eﬃciency curve in its analysis.
Together with the source dimension information, the source-to-detector distance, and the self and
external attenuation at all energies calculated by the analysis, the code can determine the mass of the
item.

The full-energy peak rate for a gamma ray of an isotope can be written as

where 𝐶𝐶(𝐸𝐸𝐸𝐸 ) = full-energy peak rate of gamma ray j with energy 𝐸𝐸𝐸𝐸 emitted from the isotope

𝜆𝜆 = decay constant of the isotope (𝜆𝜆 =ln2/𝑇𝑇1/2 , where 𝑇𝑇1/2 is the half-life)

𝑁𝑁 = number of atoms of the isotope

𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵 = branching ratio (gamma rays/disintegration) of gamma ray j from the isotope

𝜀𝜀(𝐸𝐸𝐸𝐸) = total eﬃciency for full-energy peak detection of gamma ray with energy Ej (including the intrinsic
detector eﬃciency, geometry (solid angle), item self-absorption, and attenuation in materials between the
item and detector).

Rearranging eq. 1 and expand the total eﬃciency then gives the equation

where the ﬁrst term (on the right side) corresponds to the transmission though the material itself, the
second term (inside the large parenthesis) corresponds to the transmission though the external absorbers
k, I = 𝜆𝜆 N is the activity of the isotope, 𝜀𝜀(j is the actual intrinsic detector eﬃciency at energy Ej, and

Correction is the adjustment for geometry and matrix variations. The diﬀerence of this equation (called
“true physical eﬃciency”) from the “physical eﬃciency” model is that it employs the actual detector
eﬃciency while the latter uses a generic detector eﬃciency.

FRAM simultaneously ﬁts a set of one or several equations 2, each one corresponding to the data of an
isotope, to get the activities of the isotopes. Converting the activities of these isotopes into their respective
masses then we have the combined mass of one or several isotopes. The total SNM mass is the sum of the
masses of all isotopes and is calculated from the ﬁnal isotopic composition.

b. Operation

The FRAM code was modiﬁed to incude the capability to measure SNM mass. The isotopic analysis
remained the same as that of FRAM v.6.1. Only the operation related to mass measurement is added.

Menu option: There is one new menu in the mainframe, the Detector-Source menu. Figure 3 shows the
new menu.

Clicking on the Detector-Source menu will display two options to select: Detector Info and Source Info.
Selecting the Detector Info option will display the Detector Eﬃciency dialog. Figure 4 shows an example
of the Detector Eﬃciency dialog. This dialog allows the user to enter the detector eﬃciency information.
The FRAM v.7.1 user manual [3] shows the measurement steps to obtain the eﬃciency parameters of a
detector.

Clicking on the Source Info option will display the Source Info dialog. Figure 5 shows an example of the
Source Info dialog.

The user can enter the source geometry information in this dialog. There are ﬁve diﬀerent source shapes:
point, cylinder, rectangular block, elliptical block, and ellipsoid. The geometry dimensions are for the
source and not the container. However, when the source shape is not known (such as a small source inside
a large container or a drum) then the container dimensions may be entered; the mass result will be less
accurate.

Mass option: Figure 6 shows an example of the Analyze dialog.

In addition to the Empirical Eﬃciency and Physical Eﬃciency models, there is a third eﬃciency model, the
True Physical Eﬃciency. When the user selects True Physical Eﬃciency, then the Distance text box and
Detector and Source combo-boxes appear, allowing the user to enter the source-detector distance and to
select the detector and source from the database. The source-detector distance is the distance from the
center of the source to the detector front face. A negative distance would mean the analysis uses the True
Physical Eﬃciency model to determine the isotopic composition but does not calculate the mass. A non-
negative distance will determine both the isotopic composition and mass of the item.

Clicking the Detector combo box displays the list of detectors in the database, allowing the user to select
one. Clicking the Source combo box displays the list of sources in the database, allowing the user to select
one.

If the SNM thickness and/or the container/absorber material type and thickness are known, then they can
be directly entered by clicking the Eﬃciency defaults button (see Figure 6) to open the Eﬃciency defaults
dialog and inputting them there. The minimum is the known thickness and the maximum about 20% larger.
However, this is not necessary for most measurements. The code can calculate the eﬃciency using the
built-in default SNM thickness and absorber types and thicknesses. No speciﬁc information on the item is
needed.

When mass option (i.e., non-negative distance) is selected, FRAM will calculate and display the measured
mass below the isotopic composition results. If the distance is negative, then FRAM will determine the
isotopic compositions using the true physical eﬃciency, but no mass is calculated.

c. Measurements and analysis

We used ﬁve electrically cooled HPGe detectors and the H3D CZT M400 detector to measure the items.
The HPGe detectors were: Ortec DetectiveX, 1st generation Ortec Detective, Ortec Transpec/UF6,
Canberra Cryo-Pulse 5 plus planar HPGe, and Canberra Falcon. Table 2 shows the speciﬁcations of the
detectors.

The full width at half maximum (FWHM) of the M400 appears to be about a factor of two worse than those
of the HPGe detectors. However, due to the very large low- and high-energy tails, the width of the M400
peaks at the base (full width at tenth maximum or so) is many times that of the HPGe detector peaks.

The Transpec/UF6 detector can operate in either the Transpec mode with a wide-open ﬁeld of view or in
the UF6 mode with the collimator in front of the detector to reduce the ﬁeld of view. For this detector, we
acquired data in both the wide ﬁeld of view Transpec mode and narrow ﬁeld of view UF6 mode. Figure 7
shows the pictures of the detectors used in the measurements.

More than 30 uranium, plutonium, and MOX items of wide range isotopic compositions (0.2–93% U-235,
2–26% Pu-240) and masses (4–1000 g U, 0.4–600 g Pu) were measured at various conﬁgurations by the
detectors. A minimum 0.8 mm cadmium ﬁlter was used for all plutonium and MOX measurements. Iron
(up to 22 mm thick) and lead (up to 3.2 mm thick) ﬁlters were used in many measurements in addition to
the cadmium ﬁlter (for plutonium and MOX) or nothing (for uranium measurements). The distances varied
from zero (source on contact with the detector face) to 50 cm.

For the HPGe detectors, multiple spectra of 900 seconds real time were acquired for each measurement
conﬁguration. For the M400, a list mode data set of many hours long was acquired for a measurement of
an item at a speciﬁc conﬁguration. The data set was then divided into multiple spectra of about 30 minutes
real time each.

We created new parameter sets (see section B.1.c for the M400 and sections C.1.a and C.1.b for the HPGe
detectors) for the detectors to analyze the data. We analyze the data using FRAM v.7.1 with the true
physical eﬃciency.

The FRAM results were analyzed by the ANOVA. Table 3 shows the ANOVA results of the plutonium data
sets analyzing using peaks in the energy range 120–420-keV (60–500-keV for the M400). The second
column shows the number of spectra acquired by the detector shown in the ﬁrst column and the third
column shows the average live time of each spectrum acquired by the detector. Two diﬀerent sets of
results are shown: the random uncertainty r and the item-speciﬁc systematic uncertainty 𝛿𝛿s.

Table 4 shows the ANOVA results of the plutonium data sets analyzing using peaks in the energy range
180–1010-keV (180–800-keV for the M400).

Table 5 shows the ANOVA results of the uranium data sets.

d. Observed precision vs. reported statistical uncertainties

The observed precision is the standard deviation of multiple measurements of an item. This should equal
the true statistical uncertainty of an analysis. A software code, if done correctly, should report statistical
uncertainty correctly, and this reported uncertainty should be about the same as the standard deviation.

FRAM analyzes and reports the SNM mass together with the statistical uncertainty in the short output. In
the medium and long outputs, it reports the total uncertainty that includes both the statistical uncertainty
and the long-term systematic uncertainty. The systematic uncertainty comes from the quality of the data,
such as the resolution and tails of the peaks [4]. For this mass measurement study, due to the very large
number of spectra, it is just about impossible to analyze the data in the normal mode and then examine
the results one by one. We therefore analyzed multiple data ﬁles using the /L list ﬁle in the command-line
mode (see the FRAM user manual [3]) to analyze multiple ﬁles in a single step. Unfortunately, this analysis
mode reports the isotopic composition results with both statistical uncertainties and total (statistical plus
systematic) uncertainties, but only the total uncertainty for the SNM mass. This means that we cannot
compare the observed precision with the reported statistical and must instead compare the observed
precision with the reported total error. We should expect the ratios to be somewhat smaller than unity for
proper reported uncertainty. Table 6 shows the ratios of the observed precision to that of the average total
uncertainty predicted by FRAM.

C. ISOTOPIC ANALYSIS

FRAM’s primary purpose is to analyze spectral data obtained by HPGe detectors to determine the
plutonium and uranium isotopic compositions. It is therefore important to evaluate FRAM v.7.1 in its
capability to analyze spectra of wide range isotopic compositions and acquired by good HPGe detectors.

1. Parameter sets

a. HPGe Plutonium

For plutonium analysis when using a planar HPGe detector, FRAM has most often been used to collect and
analyze data using an energy calibration of 0.075 keV/ch in the low-energy region 60–230 keV and in the
medium-energy region 120–420 keV. The most widely used mode of operation with the coaxial HPGe

detector is to acquire the spectra in the 0–1024 keV energy range at 0.125 keV/ch energy calibration. One
can then analyze data using the medium-energy region 120–420 keV or the high energy-region 180–1010
keV to analyze the data. One setback of analysis employing the medium-energy region is the summed
peaks. When there are not enough ﬁlters to signiﬁcantly suppress the 60-keV peak of 241Am (the 60-keV
peak being taller than the tallest peak in the 100-keV region), then the 60-keV peak may have a high
probability to sum with the peaks in the 100-keV region to form the summed peaks near the only 240Pu
peak at 160.3 keV. These summed peaks would aﬀect the determination of the 160.3-keV peak area and
thus the 240Pu activity. For this medium energy range analysis, it is best if the 60-keV peak height is less
than half the highest peak in the 100-keV region.

When the gamma rays below 200 keV are not available, such as when measuring a sample inside a lead-
lined container, FRAM can still obtain a complete isotopic analysis using only gamma rays in the high-
energy region 180–1010 keV. Figure 8 shows a typical plutonium spectrum with these three overlapping
analytical regions, which are depicted as three thick horizontal bars.

We created a new parameter set, HPGe_Pu_060-230, for plutonium analysis in the low energy region. This
parameter set has 0.075 keV/ch default energy calibration and requires spectra with good resolution such
as those of a planar HPGe detector.

For FRAM v.6.1 and earlier, diﬀerent parameter sets for medium-energy region analysis are used to analyze
spectra obtained by the planar HPGe detector and coaxial HPGe detector. The parameter set for the planar
detector is optimized for thin crystals and better resolution while the parameter set for the coaxial
detector is optimized for thicker detectors and somewhat worse resolution. For FRAM v.7.1, we made one
parameter set that can operate in a wide resolution range, from detectors with good resolution, such as
the planar detector, to signiﬁcantly bad resolution detectors, such as the old, degraded 1st generation
Detective (see Table 2). The parameter set has a default energy calibration of 0.125 keV/ch and is called
HPGe_Pu_120-420.

For high energy region analysis, the parameter set HPGe_Pu_180-1010 was created with default energy
calibration at 0.125 keV/ch.

b. HPGe Uranium

For uranium analysis, when using a planar HPGe detector, FRAM is normally used to collect and analyze
data in the low-energy region 60–250 keV at 0.075 keV/ch. The coaxial HPGe detector is normally used to
acquire 8K channel spectra at 0.125 keV/ch covering the energy range of 0 to 1024 keV. The analytical
region for such spectra is 120–1010 keV. Figure 9 shows an example of a uranium spectrum. The two thick
horizontal bars above the spectrum represent the two overlapping analytical regions (low and high) that
FRAM normally uses for the analysis.

We created two parameter sets to analyze low-enriched uranium (LEU) and high-enriched uranium (HEU)
employing the low-energy region and named them HPGe_ULEU_060-250 and HPGe_UHEU_060-250,
respectively. The parameter set HPGe_ULEU_060-250 can analyze uranium with enrichment below 40%
and the parameter set HPGe_UHEU_060-250 can analyze uranium with enrichment greater than 10%.

For the high-energy analytical region of the coaxial HPGe detector, the parameter sets are
HPGe_ULEU_120-1010 and HPGe_UHEU_120-1010. Like the low-energy region parameter sets, the
HPGe_ULEU_120-1010 parameter set can analyze uranium with enrichment below 40% and the
parameter set HPGe_UHEU_120-1010 can analyze uranium with enrichment greater than 10%.

The above parameter sets are the basic parameter sets that FRAM uses to analyze plutonium and uranium
isotopic compositions. All other parameter sets (such as those for non-equilibrium, heterogeneous
samples; mixed uranium and plutonium oxide (MOX); samples with other interference isotopes) are
derived from these basic ones.

c. Others

The parameter sets for the CZT and LaBr3 detectors were created from the basic parameter sets. The
CZT500_Pu_120-500 parameter set was created by modifying the HPGe_Pu_120-420 parameter set for
the broader resolution and long tail of the CZT detector. The “Medium Resolution” ﬂag is turned on to tell
FRAM to ﬁt the peaks of the CZT spectrum using the nonlinear least-squares ﬁt instead of the linear least-
squares ﬁt used with the HPGe spectra. Similarly, the LaBr_Pu_200-750 parameter set was formed by
modifying the HPGe_Pu_180-1010 parameter set for the broader resolution of the LaBr3 detector. The ﬁts
of the LaBr3 peaks are also nonlinear. We created the CZT500_U_120-1010 and LaBr_U_120-1010
parameter sets by modifying the HPGe_ULEU_120-1010 parameter set.

For the pixelated H3D CZT spectra, the parameter sets were also created from the basic parameter sets.
Because the resolution of the detector is suﬃciently good, FRAM ﬁts the peaks using linear least-square
ﬁt like that of HPGe detectors. The parameter sets also include the parameters to ﬁt of the high energy
tail of a peak. Section B.1.b showed that the measured peak area signiﬁcantly depends on how the
background regions are set. The solution to that is to adjust the branching ratios for diﬀerent sets of
background regions such the results are optimal. That also means that the branching ratios of the peaks

will not be the same as those of the HPGe detectors. Furthermore, for plutonium analysis, instead of one
parameter set for the whole wide range of isotopic composition, we created two parameter sets, one to
analyze low burnup plutonium (240Pu < 10%) and one for high burnup plutonium (240Pu > 8%). A total six
parameter sets were created for the H3D CZT detector: CZT-H3D_WGPu_060-500, CZT-H3D_RGPu_060-
500, CZT-H3D_WGPu_180-800, CZT-H3D_RGPu_180-800, CZT-H3D_ULEU_120-1010, and CZT-
H3D_UHEU_120-1010. The enrichment requirement for the CZT-H3D_ULEU_120-1010 is < 60% 235U and
for the CZT-H3D_UHEU_120-1010 is > 10% 235U.

2. Performance

We veriﬁed the performance of the parameter sets using the large archive of spectra we collected over
many years using several diﬀerent planar HPGe detectors, coaxial HPGe detectors, single crystal CZT
detectors, LaBr3detectors, and a pixelated CZT detector. The planar detectors were of various sizes, from
2.5 cm in diameter by 1.0 cm long to 5.1 cm in diameter by 1.5 cm long. The coaxial detectors also were
of various sizes, with relative eﬃciencies ranging from about 25% to 50%. These HPGe detectors were
made by either Canberra Industry or Ortec. One CZT detector was used to measure plutonium data, and
another one was used to measure uranium data. They both had the same crystal size, 500 mm3, although
the one used to measure uranium had much worse resolution than the other. Three LaBr3 detectors (one
2.5 cm in diameter by 3.8 cm long and two 5.1 cm in diameter by 1.25 cm long) were used to acquire both
plutonium and uranium data. The electronics were either standalone multichannel analyzers from
Canberra, Ortec, or GBS Elektronik or a mixture of Canberra and Ortec modules in the NIM bin racks. The
pixelated CZT detector is the M400 detector made by H3D and has 4 cm x 4 cm x 1 cm dimensions. It is a
self-contained detector system with its own data acquisition electronics.

Tables 7 and 8 show the speciﬁcations of the plutonium and uranium data measured by the HPGe planar
detectors, HPGe coaxial detectors, CZT500 detectors, LaBr3 detectors, and CZT-H3D detector.

a. Isotopic analysis results

All the plutonium spectra of the planar detectors from all the sources mentioned in Table 7 were analyzed
with the parameter sets HPGe_Pu_060-230 and HPGe_Pu_120-420. All the plutonium spectra of the
coaxial HPGe detectors, including the ones measured with lead ﬁlters, from all the sources were analyzed
with the parameter set HPGe_Pu_180-1010. These coaxial spectra, excluding those measured with lead
ﬁlters and those measured with too little ﬁlter such that the 60-keV peak signiﬁcantly larger than those in
the X-ray region, were analyzed with the parameter set HPGe_Pu_120-420.

The CZT500 and LaBr3 spectra were analyzed with the parameter set CZT500_Pu_120-500 and
LaBr_Pu_200-750, respectively. The CZT-H3D data of <10% 240Pu were analyzed with the CZT-
H3D_WGPu_180-800 parameter set and the ones with higher 240Pu concentration were analyzed with the
CZT-H3D_RGPu_180-800 parameter set.

For uranium analysis, the LEU spectra of the planar detectors were analyzed with the parameter set
HPGe_ULEU_060-250, and the HEU spectra were analyzed with the parameter set HPGe_UHEU_060-250.
Similarly, the LEU spectra of the coaxial detectors were analyzed with the parameter set HPGe_ULEU_120-
1010, and the HEU spectra were analyzed with the parameter set HPGe_UHEU_120-1010. The CZT and
LaBr3 spectra were analyzed with the parameter set CZT500_U_120-1010 and LaBr_U_120-1010,
respectively. The CZT-H3D LEU data were analyzed with the parameter sets CZT-H3D_ULEU_120-1010 and
HEU were analyze with the parameter set CZT-H3D_UHEU_120-1010. One item has enrichment of 20.1%
and its spectra (of HPGe and CZT-H3D detectors) were analyzed by both LEU and HEU parameter sets.

The physical eﬃciency model was used for all the analyses.

Each data set consists of multiple measurements taken without repositioning the sample. The individual
results for each isotope were averaged over the multiple measurements. For any isotope, the relative bias
𝑥𝑥𝑥𝑥𝑥𝑥 for each spectrum 𝑗𝑗 associated with each sample 𝑖𝑖 is

where 𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀 is the measured mass fraction and 𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷 is the declared mass fraction, which is assumed to be the
true mass fraction.

The average unweighted bias 𝑥𝑥̅𝑖𝑖 associated with sample 𝑖𝑖 is described by the equation below, where 𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 is
the number of individual measurements of sample 𝑖𝑖.

To utilize a single statistic to represent parameter set performance for a given isotope, the 𝑥𝑥̅𝑖𝑖 are further
averaged to determine the global bias 𝑥𝑥̅ represented by the equation below, where 𝑛𝑛 denotes the number
of samples measured.

The uncertainty of the unweighted global bias 𝑢𝑢𝑢𝑢̅ is the standard deviation of the average unweighted
biases 𝜎𝜎𝜎𝜎̅ divided by the square root of the number of data sets.

If all the spectral acquisitions were of items with similar isotopic compositions, of similar counting times,
and utilized similar counting geometries, then uncertainties of the reported isotopic fractions would be
similar, and the unweighted global bias should be a good indicator of overall parameter set performance.
However, as in the case of this study, diﬀerent isotopic compositions, counting times, and geometries have
resulted in diﬀerent uncertainties for each measurement. Therefore, this work also reports weighted
global biases of weighted average biases. Weights are taken as the inverse of the isotopic fraction
uncertainties reported by FRAM. Weighted global biases are calculated in a similar manner to the
unweighted global biases.

The average and uncertainty of the weighted global bias are calculated in accordance with reference [5].
The average weighted bias 𝑥𝑥̅𝑤𝑤 𝑖𝑖 associated with sample 𝑖𝑖 is

Here the weight 𝑤𝑤𝑤𝑤𝑤𝑤 is the inverse of the reported uncertainty for each spectrum 𝑗𝑗 associated with each
sample 𝑖𝑖.

The global bias 𝑥𝑥̅𝑤𝑤 is represented by the equation

Here, wi is the weight of data set i and is set to be the inverse of the standard deviation of the results of
data set i.

And ﬁnally, the uncertainty of the weighted global bias 𝑢𝑢𝑢𝑢 𝑥𝑥̅ is

Tables 9 and 10 present the plutonium and uranium bias results of the analyses, respectively.

The unweighted bias and weighted bias of all the isotopes, except 238Pu, of all detectors are small and
generally within two sigma (i.e. less than twice the uncertainty). For the 238Pu, due to the very small
fraction for low burnup plutonium, the relative uncertainty of the determined 238Pu fraction is very large.
The large uncertainty means that the measured 238Pu result is widely scattered and this will signiﬁcantly
aﬀect the unweighted average bias. The widely scattered result does not aﬀect the weighted bias because
they also have large uncertainties, which means small weights and only marginally aﬀect the average bias.
Furthermore, the weighted bias appears to have smaller uncertainty than the unweighted bias for virtually
all the results. This means that the weighted bias is a better indicator of the accuracy of the results.

Like 238Pu, the 234U fraction is very small for natural and depleted uranium, and its result has a large
uncertainty and is widely scattered. The inclusion of these results would signiﬁcantly aﬀect the unweighted
average bias but not the weighted bias. The 234U bias results of the HPGe detectors show this eﬀect. The
eﬀect somehow is not present for the CZT and LaBr3 detectors, because, for these detectors, 234U is
determined by correlation instead of direct measurement and does not behave like the directly measured
234
 U of HPGe detectors.

The analysis of very high enriched uranium (> 90% 235U) employing the low-energy region also produces
238
 U with very large relative uncertainty. Similar to the 238Pu and 234U situations above, the inclusion of
these 238U results would signiﬁcantly aﬀect the unweighted bias of 238U.

For most isotopes and parameter sets, global biases agree with zero within uncertainty (typically within
two sigma, or 95% conﬁdence intervals). Also, for each parameter set, the weighted bias uncertainties are
almost always less than the unweighted bias uncertainties, like that of plutonium analysis.

For both the plutonium and uranium analyses, the bias uncertainties of the CZT500, LaBr3, and pixelated
CZT detectors are about an order of magnitude worse than the uncertainties of the HPGe detector’s
analyses. That is also expected since the resolution of these detectors and the tail of the CZT500 and CZT-
H3D detectors are much worse than those of the HPGe detector.

b. ANOVA analysis

The isotopic results are also analyzed by the ANOVA. Tables 11 and 12 present the plutonium and uranium
ANOVA analysis results respectively. For the analysis of each parameter set, two diﬀerent sets of results
are shown: the random uncertainty 𝛿𝛿R and the item-speciﬁc systematic uncertainty 𝛿𝛿S.

The plutonium results appear to be as expected, with the analysis of the planar HPGe data using the low
energy region analysis giving the best results for the two major isotopes 239Pu and 240Pu, the analysis of all
HPGe data using the middle energy region analysis being second best, and the analysis using the highest
energy region being third best. The results of other detectors are about a factor of 3–10 worse.

The 235U results are also similar to those of plutonium, with the low energy region analysis of planar HPGe
detector data giving the best results and high energy region analysis of coaxial HPGe detector data being
second best. The 238U results of the low-energy region analysis are not good because the very large
uncertainties of very high enriched uranium signiﬁcantly increase both the random and systematic
uncertainties 𝛿𝛿R and 𝛿𝛿S.

Examining the results of the CZT and LaBr3 detectors in detail, we see that the random uncertainties R
and the item-speciﬁc systematic uncertainties S of these medium resolution detectors are about a factor
of 3–10 larger than those of the coaxial HPGe detector in their respected analysis energy ranges. This

factor of 3–10 worse is similar to the factor of 10 worse of the global biases in the previous section and is
expected.

c. Comparison of observed precision and reported statistical uncertainties

FRAM analyzes and reports both the isotopic results and the corresponding statistical uncertainties (only
propagating uncertainty due to Poisson counting statistics). It is of interest to see how the average
reported statistical uncertainty of an isotopic fraction compares with the observed precision (standard
deviation of reported isotopic fractions) for many runs. Table 13 shows the ratios of the observed precision
to that of the average statistical uncertainty predicted by FRAM for plutonium data.

The ratios are about unity for most of the isotopes for the HPGe detectors. This means that the FRAM
reported statistical uncertainties for the HPGe detector are reasonably close to representing the precisions
of the analysis. For the CZT detector, FRAM underestimates the uncertainty component from radiation-
counting statistics somewhat. For the LaBr3 detector, the ratios for 238Pu, 239Pu, 240Pu, and 241Am are much
less than unity. This is due to the many bounds we employ in the LaBr3 analysis. These bounds constrain
the results in speciﬁc directions, making the analysis appear to be more repeatable.

Table 14 shows the ratios of the observed uncertainty to that of the average uncertainty predicted by
FRAM for uranium data.

The ratios of the CZT and LaBr3 detectors are much greater than unity for all isotopes. This means that
FRAM underestimates the uncertainties of the CZT and LaBr3 analysis. So, for CZT and LaBr3 analysis of
uranium data, it is better to use the reported total uncertainties in the medium results output than to use
the reported random uncertainties in the short results output. The reported total uncertainty includes the
systematic uncertainty in addition to the statistical uncertainty. Reference [4] describes the FRAM
systematic uncertainties in detail.

D. CONCLUSION

We have introduced two major improvements of FRAM version 7.1: analyzing spectra of pixelated CZT
detectors and measuring SNM mass. We measured spectra of more than 30 uranium, plutonium, and MOX
items at various conﬁgurations by ﬁve electrically cooled HPGe detectors and the pixelated CZT detector.
We upgraded the FRAM code to ﬁt the high energy tail of the pixelated CZT peaks. Even though FRAM
v.7.1 can analyze the pixelated CZT spectra, the isotopic composition results are much worse than those
of the HPGe detectors, about a factor of 3–10 worse. We also added capability to measure the SNM mass.
The total accuracy of the mass results of the electrically cooled HPGe detectors is about 10–20%
uncertainty and of the pixelated CZT detector is about 30%.

We analyzed the archival data (of planar HPGe, coaxial HPGe, single crystal CZT, and LaBr detector) and
the pixelated CZT detector data for isotopic composition performance. We created new parameter sets
for FRAM v.7.1. These parameter sets were tested with thousands of spectra to determine their accuracy
at predicting the isotopic composition of plutonium and uranium. Their performances were reasonable
and expected. These parameter sets were also the same ones used to measure mass.

FRAM was also tested for its accuracy on reporting the uncertainties of the results. The reported
uncertainties for the HPGe detector are about right for both plutonium and uranium analyses. The
reported uncertainties of the LaBr3 detector for plutonium analysis appear to be very much overestimated.
The appearance of overestimation of the LaBr3 uncertainties was due to the observed uncertainties, which
were too small. The too-small observed uncertainties were in turn the result of the way the analysis was
done in which the 240Pu fraction were constrained to within a small range. The reported uncertainties of
both the CZT and LaBr3 detectors for uranium analysis are signiﬁcantly underestimated. We suggest that
for uranium analysis with CZT and LaBr3 data, the reported total uncertainties should be used instead of
the statistical uncertainties in reporting the uncertainties.

This work was supported by the U.S. Department of Energy, Oﬃce of International Nuclear Safeguards
(NA-241) and Oﬃce of Nuclear Veriﬁcation (NA-243).

Task 2: FRAM Version 7.1’s Bias

 The Fixed-Energy Response-Function Analysis with Multiple Eﬃciency (FRAM) code was developed at
Los Alamos National Laboratory to measure the gamma-ray spectrometry of the isotopic composition of
plutonium, uranium, and other actinides. For FRAM versions 4 and earlier, the reported uncertainties of
the results come from the propagation of the statistics in the peak areas only. No systematic error
components are included in the reported uncertainties. For FRAM versions 5 and 6, we examined the
FRAM analytical results of both the archival plutonium data and the data speciﬁcally acquired for the
isotopic uncertainty analysis project and found the relationship between the bias and other parameters.
We worked out the equations representing the biases of the measured isotopes from each measurement
using internal spectral parameters, such as peak resolution and shape, region of analysis, and burnup
(for plutonium) or enrichment (for uranium). The resulting biases were included in the reported
uncertainties of FRAM v.5 and v.6.

For the FRAM version 7.1, we are doing the same study that we did for FRAM v.5 and v.6 The resulting
biases are included in its reported total uncertainties.

A. INTRODUCTION

The Fixed-Energy Response-Function Analysis with Multiple Eﬃciency (FRAM) software was developed
and continues to be reﬁned by Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL). The code was developed for
gamma-ray spectrometry measurements of the isotopic composition of plutonium, uranium, and other
actinides [1–3]. In 2005, we studied FRAM’s bias as a function of peak resolution and shape, intending to
apply the results to the upgraded version of FRAM [4]. The software in that study was FRAM version 4. In
2011, we did another bias study on FRAM version 5 [5] and applied the bias function to the FRAM v.5
code. In 2019, we did a bias study on FRAM version 6 [6] and applied the bias function to the FRAM v.6
code. In that study, we used diﬀerent bias formulae than those in v.5. For the bias study of FRAM v.7, we
use the same bias formulae as those of FRAM v.6.

In the 2005 study, experiments were set up to obtain spectra of various peak resolutions and shapes to
study the relationships of the biases with the spectra’s internal parameters (peak resolution and shape,
region of analysis, plutonium burnup, or uranium enrichment). The same data were used for the FRAM
v.5 study in 2011 and FRAM v.6 study in 2019. For the study reported here, we used the same spectra as
in the previous studies.

B. PLUTONIUM (HPGe)

FRAM can analyze a spectrum using any energy region, including the very narrow or very wide energy
region, if the region contains the peaks of every isotope. The disadvantage of a very small region analysis
is that the statistics may not be very good, since the analysis uses only a small number of peaks in a small
region. The disadvantage of a very large region analysis is that, due to the very wide energy range, the
eﬃciency curve may not be completely represented by the eﬃciency models used by FRAM. This will
lead to a bad eﬃciency curve, which will then lead to bad results.

FRAM normally analyzes a plutonium spectrum using one of the three energy regions: low energy (60–
230 keV), medium energy (120–420 keV), and high energy (180–1010 keV). Figure 1 shows a typical

plutonium spectrum with these three overlapping analytical regions, which are depicted with three thick
horizontal bars.

1. Data acquisition

The spectra were acquired with the electronics adjusted so that each set would have distinctive peak
resolutions and shapes. Two detector systems were used for the experiments, one planar germanium
detector system and one coaxial germanium detector system. The planar detector system consisted of a
16-mm-diameter  13-mm-long planar detector from Canberra and the DSPEC Plus multichannel
analyzer (MCA) from Ortec. The coaxial detector system consisted of a 58-mm-diameter  53-mm-long
coaxial detector (32% relative eﬃciency) and the DSPEC Plus MCA, also from Ortec.

The samples for these measurements included four of the seven samples from the plutonium isotopic
determination inter-comparison exercise (PIDIE) set: PIDIE-1, PIDIE-3, PIDIE-5, and PIDIE-7. These
samples were small, only 0.4 g each. In the planar detector system, the input rates for the four samples
were 3, 5, 8, and 10 kHz, respectively, from low to high burnup. For the coaxial detector system, the
input rates were 16 kHz for the PIDIE-1 sample and 20 kHz for the other three samples.

The data for the planar detector were acquired in 8-K channels at 0.075 keV/ch up to >600 keV so that
the data could be analyzed in two diﬀerent energy ranges: 60–230 keV (low energy) and 120–500 keV
(medium energy). For the coaxial detector, the spectra were acquired in 8K channels at 0.125 keV/ch,
covering the entire 0–1,024-keV energy range. These spectra can be analyzed using two separate
parameter sets employing the 120–500-keV (medium energy) and 180–1,010-keV (high energy) regions.

In both detector systems, we varied the rise time of the DSPEC Plus to obtain spectra with various
resolutions. The rise times used were 0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 1.0, 1.4, 2.0, 2.8, 4.0, and 8.0 μs. The ﬂattop was 1.0
μs, and the cusp value was 0.8. The acquisition time for each spectrum was 15 minutes live time, and 16
spectra were obtained for each dataset. The full-width at half-maximum (FWHM) at 208 keV for the
planar detector varied from about 0.64 keV at 8-μs rise time to 0.82 keV at 0.2-μs rise time; for the
coaxial detector, the variation was from 1.04 keV to 2.28 keV for the same span of rise times. The peak
tails were all small for these measurements. In general, the FWHM is larger for higher-energy peaks and
smaller for lower-energy peaks.

To obtain spectra with various shapes, we used a rise time of 4.0 μs and manually adjusted the pole zero
(PZ) to produce peaks with low-energy tails of various sizes. For each sample, six sets of data, with 16
spectra (15 minutes true time) in each set, were obtained, with the peak tail percentages varying from
approximately zero to about 17%. For a spectrum, the tail of a peak can be either larger or smaller at
diﬀerent energy. On average, the tail is about the same at all diﬀerent energies.

2. Analysis

a. Correlation

The bias correlation for an isotope is

                              Bias = a · Fb · (W · (1 + c · T) )d       (Eq. 1)

where bias = |Measured/Accepted – 1|; a, b, c, and d are variables, with a, c, and d being nonnegative; F
is the isotopic percent of the isotope; W is the FWHM (keV) of the selected peak; and T is the tail percent
of that selected peak. For 241Am, the isotopic percent is deﬁned as 100 times the ratio of 241Am to
plutonium. The selected peaks are chosen to be the 129-keV peak of 239Pu for the low-energy region
analysis, the 208-keV peak of 241Pu and 241Am for the medium-energy region analysis, and the 662-keV
peak of 241Am for the high-energy-region analysis.

b. Bias ﬁtting

To obtain the bias for any measurement, we would need to ﬁt the data points to a model, which is
described by Equation 1. Normally, when a curve is ﬁtted through some data points, half of the points,
on average, will be above the curve and half will be below. Here, we are trying to obtain a curve that
would represent the standard deviation or the bias of the data. For a Gaussian distribution, about 32% of
the points will be outside one standard deviation, or sigma, and 68% will be inside. So, in ﬁtting Equation
1, we gave the points above the curve a weight of 0.68, and the points below the curve a weight of 0.32.
Then the bias curve is obtained such that roughly 32% of the points are above it and 68% are below it. In
addition to the 32/68 weight distribution, each data point is given a weight equal to the inverse of the
statistical error of that data point.

For the low-energy-region analysis, we use the parameter set HPGe_Pu_060-230 to analyze the data of
both planar and coaxial detectors. For the medium-energy-region analysis, we use the parameter set
HPGe_Pu_120-420 to also analyze the data of both planar and coaxial detectors. For the high-energy-
region analysis, the parameter set HPGe_Pu_180-1010 was used to analyze the coaxial data. The physical
eﬃciency model was used for these analyses.

We grouped the average results (of 16 runs at each setup) based on the energy region used in the
analysis, regardless of the detector type: low-energy, medium-energy, and high-energy. We ﬁtted the
data from each group to Equation 1 to obtain the values for a, b, c, and d.

c. Results

Table 1 shows the results of the ﬁts.

We see that if the widths of the peaks are not zero, some bias will always be associated with the ﬁnite
resolutions. (The tail can be made zero or very near zero with a good detector system and careful
measurements.)

The resolution for a typical planar detector is about 0.55 keV FWHM at 129 keV and 0.7 keV FWHM at
208 keV. For a typical coaxial detector, the FWHM is about 1 keV at 208 keV and 1.45 keV at 662 keV. The
tails are about 1% for peaks at all energies. Figure 2 shows the plots of the biases calculated using such
FWHM and tail parameters.

C. URANIUM (HPGe)

Figure 3 shows an example of a uranium spectrum with two regions, one below the K-edge and one
above the K-edge, separated by the dashed line. The two thick horizontal bars above the spectrum
represent the two overlapping analytical regions (low and high) that FRAM normally uses for the
analysis.

1. Data acquisition

The data acquisition system was set up the same way as it was for the plutonium bias determination
(Section B). For these measurements, ﬁve low-enriched uranium (LEU) samples of the NBS-SRM*

* NBS-SRM - National Bureau of Standards - Standard Reference Materials. 969 set and three highly
enriched uranium (HEU) samples of the NBL-CRM†

† NBL-CRM - New Brunswick Laboratory - Certiﬁed Reference Materials. 146 set, ranging from 0.3% to
93.2% 235U enrichment, were used. These samples weighed about 200 g each. For the planar detector
system, the input rates for the ﬁve LEU samples were small, ranging from 1.8 kHz for the 0.3% 235U
sample to 3.5 kHz for the 4.5% 235U sample. For the three HEU samples, input rates were at 10 kHz. For
the coaxial detector system, the input rates were about 20 kHz for all eight samples.

The data for the planar detector were acquired in 4-K channels at 0.075 keV/ch and analyzed using the
peaks in the 60-keV to 210-keV energy range. For the coaxial detector, the spectra were acquired in 8-K
channels at 0.125 keV/ch and analyzed using the parameter set employing the 120-keV to 1,010-keV
region.

Just as we did for plutonium (Section B), we varied the rise time of the DSPEC Plus to obtain spectra with
various resolutions for both detector systems. The rise times used were 0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 1.0, 1.4, 2.0, 2.8,

4.0, and 8.0 μs. The ﬂattop was 1.0 μs, and the cusp value was 0.8. The acquisition time for each
spectrum was 15 minutes of live time. The FWHM at 186 keV for the planar detector varied from about
0.61 keV at 8-μs rise time to 0.77 keV at 0.2-μs rise time, and for the coaxial detector, it was from 1.00
keV to 2.32 keV for the same span of rise times. The peak tails were all small for these measurements.

To obtain spectra with various shapes, we used a rise time of 4.0 μs and manually adjusted the PZ to
produce peaks with low-energy tails of various sizes. For each sample, six sets of data, with 16 spectra
(15 minutes true time) for each set, were obtained, with the 186-keV peak-tail percents varying from
approximately zero to about 15%.

2. Analysis

For the low-energy-region analysis, we analyzed the planar and coaxial data using the GePlnr_ULEU_060-
250 and GePlnr_UHEU_060-250 parameter sets. For the high-energy-region analysis, we analyzed the
coaxial data using the GeCoax_ULEU_120-1010 and GeCoax_UHEU_120-1010 parameter sets. The
eﬃciency model for these analyses was the physical model.

a. Correlation

The correlation equation relating the resolutions and tails of the peaks to the bias of the uranium
isotopes is the same as that of plutonium. It is Equation 1,

Bias = a · Fb · (W · (1 + c · T ) )d,

where bias = |Measured/Accepted – 1|; a, b, c, and d are variables, with a, c, and d being nonnegative; F
is the isotopic percent of the isotope; W is the FWHM (keV) of the selected peak; and T is the tail percent
of that selected peak. The selected peak is chosen to be the 186-keV peak of 235U for both low- and high-
energy region analyses.The bias ﬁttings of Equation 1 for 234U, 235U, and 238U are done the same way as
for plutonium, as described in Section B.2.

b. Results

Table 2 shows the results of the ﬁts.

Like the plutonium analysis, we see
that if the widths of the peaks are
not zero, some bias will always be
associated with the ﬁnite resolutions.

The typical FWHM at 186 keV is
about 0.65 keV for the planar

D. NON-HPGE

We obtained a limited number of spectra with two LaBr3 detectors, two 500-mm3 CZT detectors, and a
large (16-cm3), pixelized CZT detector (made by H3D). The spectra were of many certiﬁed plutonium and
uranium items we have in our group at LANL. For the LaBr3 detectors, the average FWHM at 186 keV was
10.5 keV and at 662 keV was 19.3 keV. The peak tail percentage was about zero. One CZT detector was
used to measure uranium with an average 4.5 keV FWHM and 22.6% peak tail at 186 keV. Another CZT
detector was used to measure plutonium. At 208 keV, the average FWHM was 4.6 keV and the average
low energy tail was 16.0%. For the pixelized CZT detector, the average FWHM was 2.6 keV and total (low
energy plus high energy) tail was 13.5% at 186 keV and 3.6 keV FWHM and 34% total tail at 662 keV.

We analyzed the spectra using the corresponding parameter sets LaBr_Pu_200-750, LaBr_U_120-1010,
CZT500_Pu_120-500, CZT500_U_120-1010, CZT-H3D_PuRG_060-500, CZT-H3D_PuWG_060-500, CZT-
H3D_PuRG_180-800, CZT-H3D_PuWG_180-800, CZT-H3D_ULEU_120-1010, and CZT-H3D_UHEU_120-
1010. The measured biases were then compared with the biases calculated from Equation 1. Table 3
shows the measured-bias to calculated-bias ratios. These ratios are entered in the user-designated
systematic component for the isotopes in FRAM (under the command syst_error_xxyyy in the
Application Constants section of the FRAM parameter set, where xxyyy is the isotope name).

E. CONCLUSION

We have studied the bias of FRAM analysis by employing various parameter sets using gamma rays and
x-rays in various energy regions of data taken with the HPGe, LaBr3, single crystal CZT, and pixelized CZT
detectors. We determined the biases as functions of the resolutions and the tails of the peaks. This
method considers the speciﬁc measurement conditions for every measurement and estimates the bias
based on those measurement conditions.

FRAM v.7.1 includes the systematic uncertainties in addition to the random uncertainties in its results.
These systematic uncertainties are based on the biases shown in this paper. The results, with the
systematic uncertainties, are shown in the medium and long display modes of FRAM.

REFERENCES

1. T.E. Sampson, G.W. Nelson, and T.A. Kelley, “FRAM: A Versatile Code for Analyzing the Isotopic

report LA-11720-MS (1989).

the FRAM Software,” Los Alamos National Laboratory report LA-14018 (2003).
