 Appendix D. ASCII File Description

     You can save spectral data in a text file by selecting the File | Save As. . . option then choosing ASCII as the
 File Format.
 The first 48 lines of such a text file are shown on the next page. This was produced by opening the file
     u0071pl.spe and then performing a File | Save As. . . with the File Format: ASCII. The file format is in two
 columns, ch,counts, separated by a comma. The resulting text file was opened with Microsoft Word, and
 then the first page was printed.
 The first line in the output file displays the name of the original file (before conversion to ASCII) in which the
 data resides. The next two lines display information that is usually found in the header portion of the spectral
 data file. In the second line, between the commas, are numbers representing the date and time associated with
 that spectrum. The numbers, in order, indicate the year, month, day, hour, minute, second, millisecond, and time
 zone offset. The third line indicates the live time and true time used in collecting the spectral data. The fourth
 line is blank. The fifth line consists of the string “ch” followed by the comma separator character followed by
 the string “counts.” The remaining lines present the spectral data with a channel number, comma, followed by a
 count.
 When you choose to open a data file and select ASCII as the storage format, FRAM attempts to read the header
 information: date, time, live time, and true time. If this fails, it proceeds to the spectral data. Thus, the header
 information in lines 1-3 need not be present in the file. But the line with the strings “ch” and “counts” must
 be present. FRAM looks for the character between these two strings and assumes that this will be the delimiter
 between each column of numbers in the succeeding lines. As the channel counts are being read, they are
 checked against what they ought to be in order to ensure the integrity of the data.

Appendix E. FRAM Command Line Operation

 E.1. Introduction
 When FRAM is run with a string argument it will switch to the command-line operation mode. This mode
 of operation will execute the commands supplied in the argument and when the analysis is completed will
 automatically terminate. It will not open a window.
 The Command-Line Operation mode is meant for users who want to incorporate FRAM into their own
 applications. This mode allows the user to make a very simple interface to the FRAM analysis engine.
 In the command-line mode, FRAM will not APPEND the one-line results to the Uranium7.csv or
 Plutonium7.csv file as in the normal operation mode. Instead, it will WRITE the one-line results to the
 FRAMcmd.log file. This file resides in the database directory.

 E.2. Command Descriptions
 These commands work with FRAM v.5, v.6, and v.7.
 • /I Init File Open the INI file and read the commands in the file. The default directory is
 the directory containing the FRAM database, C:\FRAMdata\FRAM71.

 • /p Parameter set Required. Parameter set must be in the FRAM database. Parameter sets'
 names are case sensitive.

 • /i Input file Required. The default directory is C:\FRAMdata\Spectra. The
 commands /i Uran\file1.txt is equivalent to /i
 C:\FRAMdata\Spectra\Uran\file1.txt. If there is "space" in the file name or
 directory then the whole file name, including the directory, must be
 enclosed between double quotes. The inclusion of space in the filename is
 discouraged.

 • /o Output file The default is NULL. The default directory is C:\FRAMdata\Results.
 The commands /o Uran\file1.res is equivalent to /o
 C:\FRAMdata\Results\Uran\file1.res. If there is "space" in the file name or
 directory then the whole file name, including the directory, must be
 enclosed between double quotes. The inclusion of space in the filename is
 discouraged.

 • /f Input file format If the input file has the extension chn, spc, mca, cnf, spe, or txt then the file
 format will be automatically assigned to the Ortec chn, Ortec spc, Canberra
 S100, Canberra Cam, IAEA MMCA, or FRAM Ascii, respectively. If the
 input file format is not one of those then this /f command option is required.
 The acceptable formats are chn, spc, mca, cnf, spe, and txt. The /f
 command option will override the file format extracted from the extension
 of the input file name.

 • /e Rel. Efficiency 0 or 1. 0 means Physical model and 1 means Empirical model. The default is 0.

 • /c Pu242/U236 Real number. A negative number means that the 242Pu or 236U value is

 169

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

 determined by correlation. A value from 0 to less than 100 represents the
 operator declared %Pu242 or %U236. A value greater than or equal 100
 means that the 242Pu or 236U value is determined by measurement. The
 default is -1.

 • /t Pu242/U236 declared date Default is NULL, which means same date the spectrum was acquired.
 The date must be in either the international "yyyy-mm-dd" format or the
 American "dd-Mmm-yyyy" format.

 • /s U separation date Default is "1900-01-01". The date must be in either the international "yyyy-
 mm-dd" format or the American "dd-Mmm-yyyy" format.

 • /r U separation time Default is "12:00" (noon).

 • /a Auto Analysis 0 or 1. 0 stands for normal analysis and 1 stands for auto analysis. The
 default is 0.

 • /g E calibration gain The default is the energy calibration gain from the parameter set.

 • /z E calibration zero The default is the energy calibration intercept (or zero) from the parameter
 set.

 • /m Prev. Calorimetry The sample power (Watts) of the previous calorimetric measurement. The
 default is NULL. This command will add an extra line containing the
 calorimetric measurement information and the resulted plutonium or
 uranium mass to the FRAMcmd.log file.

 • /n Prev. Neutron The effective 240Pu mass (grams) of the previous neutron measurement. The
 default is NULL. This command will add an extra line containing the
 neutron measurement information and the resulted plutonium mass to the
 FRAMcmd.log file. If both commands /m and /n are present, then /n will be
 ignored.

 • /u Prev. Uncertainty The one sigma uncertainty for either previous calorimetric measurement or
 neutron measurement. The unit is either Watt or gram. The default is 0.0.

 • /d Prev. Meas. Date The date of the previous calorimetric measurement or neutron measurement.
 The date must be in either the ISO 8601 international format (yyyy-mm-dd)
 or the American format (dd-Mmm-yyyy). The default date is the current
 date.

 • /x Hide Results 0 or 1 where 0 means the results are written to the standard DBI files
 (releff.dbi, pcfram.dbi, etc.) and 1 means no results are written to the DBI
 files; only one-line results are written to the FRAMcmd.log file the end of
 the analysis. This command also invalidates the /o command. The default is
 0.

 170

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

 The order of the commands is not important. The command
           FRAM /i FRAM.spe /p HPGe_Pu_120-420 /e 1
 is equivalent to
 FRAM /I FRAMcmd.ini
 where FRAMcmd.ini is a text file containing the following statements:
 [CmdLineConfig]
 /i = FRAM.spe
           /p = HPGe_Pu_120-420
 /e = 1

 In the example above, FRAM will analyze the file C:\FRAMdata\Spectra\FRAM.spe using the parameter
 set HPGe_Pu_120-420, the empirical efficiency model, the IAEA MMCA file format, and the correlation
 for the determination of 242Pu. The statement [CmdLineConfig] must be at the beginning, before any
 command line.
 For every analysis, the full results are saved in the pcfram.dbi file and the pcfauto.dbi (if auto analysis is
 used) file and the short results (1 line) is saved in the FRAMcmd.log file. All these 3 files reside in the
 directory containing the FRAM database. If the /o option is used, then the full results are also saved in the
 specified output file.
 The commands below work only with FRAM v.6.1 and v.7.1.
 • /b BG file Subtract the background peaks using a background spectrum. The default
 directory is C:\FRAMdata\Spectra.

 • /f Input file format Specify the file format for the input file. In FRAM v.5, if /f is not present, the
 input file must have the extension chn, spc, mca, cnf, spe, or txt, and the file
 format will be automatically assigned to Ortec chn, Ortec spc, Canberra S100,
 Canberra Cam, IAEA MMCA, or FRAM Ascii, respectively. In version 6.1
 and 7.1, if /f is not present, FRAM will read the file using the Auto format.

 • /j N42 spectrum # The spectrum number of the N42 file. It is used together with /f n42
 command. The default value is 1.

 • /L List file List file containing rows of one-line commands such as “/i inputfile.spe /p
 param”. This command allows analysis of multiple files in command line
 mode.
 The command

 FRAM /L FRAMlist.txt.spe

 is equivalent to

           FRAM /i FRAM.spe /p HPGe_Pu_120-420 /e 1
           FRAM /i FRAM.spe /p HPGe_Pu_180-1010

 171

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

 where FRAMlist.txt is a text file containing the following statements:

           /i FRAM.spe /p                   HPGe_Pu_120-420 /e 1
           /i FRAM.spe /p                   HPGe_Pu_180-1010

 These commands work with FRAM v.7.1 only.
 • /e Rel. Efficiency -1, 0, or 1. -1 means true physical model, 0 means Physical model, and 1
 means Empirical model. The default is 0. The is the same as the /e of
 FRAM v.6.1 with the addition of the True Physical model.

 • /k Detector Number Positive integer. The detector number is in the list of detectors in Detector-
                                                     Source | Detector Info.

 • /l Source Number Positive integer. The source number is in the list of the sources in
                                                     Detector-Source | Source Info.

 • /y Det-Source Dist. Real number. A negative number means that no mass calculations are
 perform. A non-negative value indicates the distance from the source center
 to the front face of the detector and the mass of the item will be determined.
 The default is -1.

 • /h Det-Source Dist. Err. Non-negative real number. The uncertainty of the Detector-Source distance.
 The default is 0.

 172

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

Appendix G. Nuclear Data
 FRAM uses nuclear data in its various calculations.
 The nuclear data (half-lives and specific powers) for the plutonium isotopes and 241Am used in the ISOPOW
 decay corrections comes from ENSDF database of the NNDC. These values are hard-wired into the FRAM
     software. The Measure | Compare option uses these same hardwired values.
 Each parameter set also contains user editable values for half-life, Atomic Mass, Specific Power, and the 240Pueff
 coefficient. The Built-In parameter sets use the ENSDF values for the plutonium isotopes. The uranium half-
 lives come from the 8th Ed of the Table of Isotopes. The uranium specific powers are calculated from the decay
 schemes.
 The hard-wired values in ISOPOW and the values for the same parameters in the parameter set are not
 connected. Changing a value in the parameter set will not affect the same parameter in ISOPOW.
     The initial values in the Built-In parameter sets and the hardwired values in ISOPOW and Measure |
 Compare are the same. The two screens below display this data for the most encountered isotopes.

 Isotope data in the HPGe_Pu_180-1010 Built-In parameter set.

 Isotope data in the HPGe_UHEU_120-1010 Built-In parameter set.

 182

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

Appendix H. Detector and Source Information for Mass Measurement

 H.1. Detector Information
           Selecting the Source-Detector | Detector Info option will display the Detector Efficiency dialog.

 Scrolling the horizontal bar in the Detector Efficiency dialog to the right will show the values of the
 constants k–n of the angular efficiency.

 183

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

 This dialog allows the user to enter the detector efficiency information. The equation for the detector
 efficiency is
           ln(𝑒𝑓𝑓@1𝑚) = 𝑎𝐿2 + 𝑏𝐿 + 𝑐 + 𝑑𝐿−1 + 𝑒𝐿−2 + 𝑓𝐿−3 + 𝑔𝐿−4                                           (1)
 where the distance 1 m is from the source center to the detector face and L=ln(Energy in KeV). The Center
 value in the Detector Efficiency dialog is the distance from the detector face to the effective center of the
 detector, and a through g are the constants of the detector efficiency.
 The figure below shows the generic detector-source configuration.

 The gamma-ray flux F entering the detector is
 𝑐 𝑐
           𝐹 = 𝐷2 = (𝑑+𝑥)2                                 (2)

 where c is a constant related to the source activity, D is the distance from the effective center of the source
 to the effective center of the detector, d is the distance from the effective center of the source to the front
 surface of the detector, and x is the distance from front surface of the detector to the effective center of the
 detector.
 Measuring a calibration check source at two different distances will allow the determination of the activity
 c and the distance x. To minimize the effect of the detector’s size, the distance d of the nearer measurement
 should be at least 3 times the detector diameter, and the distance of the farther measurement should be at
 least twice the closer one. The value x, marked Center in the Detector Efficiency dialog, starts small for
 low-energy gamma rays and slowly increases as the gamma-ray energy increases. The Center values
 shown in the Detector Efficiency box above were the averages of the 344-, 779-, and 964-keV peaks of
 152
 Eu. A single gamma ray at 662 keV of 137Cs is comparable to the average of these three 152Eu peaks and
 can be used for this purpose.
 A single source, such as 152Eu, or a set of multiple sources with gamma rays spread out over a wide energy
 range may be measured at a specific distance. The peak rates of the peaks are obtained. The efficiency of a
 peak at the measured distance is the peak rate divided by the source activity and the photoemission
 probability (or the branching ratio) of that gamma-ray. The efficiency of a peak at distance d=1 m is then
 calculated using equation 2. The equation for the detector efficiency at all energy levels is then obtained by
 fitting the efficiency of various peaks at 1 m to equation 1.
 The angular efficiency is determined by measuring a check source along a line perpendicular to the detector
 direction. The figure below shows an example of the measurements for the angular efficiency.

 184

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

 The angular efficiency Y is determined by equation
           𝑌 = 𝑘𝑋 4 + 𝑙𝑋 3 + 𝑚𝑋 2 + 𝑛𝑋                     (3)
 Where X = S/D, where S is the distance along the line perpendicular to the detector axis and D is the
 distance from the detector effective center to the source line; and Y = ln(I/I0), where I0 is the peak rate at
 location S0 and I is the peak rate at other locations.
 The first five detectors shown in the Detector Efficiency dialog are electrically cooled HPGe detectors
 and the 6th detector is the pixelized CZT detector made by H3D. All these detectors have tungsten side
 shielding to attenuate the unwanted gamma rays entering the detectors from the sides. The side shielding
 for the first four detectors are standard and came with the detectors. The shields for the last two detectors
 are custom made.
 The two detectors Trans-SPEC and Trans-SPEC UF6 are the same detector except that the UF6
 detector has a tungsten collimator in front to reduce the field of view.
 The file C:\Framdata\References\Detector_Calibration_Procedure_Example.xlsx
 shows the examples of the detector center, the detector efficiency, and the angular detector efficiency.

 H.2. Source information
           Selecting the Source-Detector | Source Info option will display the Source Info dialog. You can
 enter the source geometry information in this dialog.

 185

____________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________

 The Source Info dialog above shows the source information of several common items: the SRM-969 (set
 of five 200g LEU oxides), CRM-146 (set of three 230g HEU oxides), PIDIE (set of seven 0.4g plutonium
 oxides), and CBNM NRM-271 (set of 4 6g plutonium oxides). The geometry dimensions are for the source
 and not the container.
 For a measurement of one or several small sources with unknown dimensions inside a large container such
 as a 55-gallon waste drum, the container dimensions may be entered. You should estimate the fill level of
 the materials inside the container and use it for the height instead of the actual container height. The
 uncertainty to be entered into the ± (uncertainty) box is

 𝑊2 𝐻2 2
 𝑒𝑟𝑟 = √0.0034 ( + ) + 0.12𝐷2 /𝑛, where W, H, and D are the container’s width, height, and depth,
 𝐿 𝐿
 L is the distance from the effective center of the detector to the container center, and n is the number of
 measurements around the container. If the container is rotated while being measured, then n can be set to
 infinity.
 There are five source shapes: point, cylinder, elliptical block, rectangular block, and ellipsoid. The cylinder
 represents the material inside an upright can with the horizontal detector pointing at it. The shape of the can
 bottom can be either circular or elliptical. The elliptical block represents a disk with the detector looking
 through the depth of the disk. If a source is irregular, then you can try to use an allowed shape that is
 closest to the source shape and add some extra distance uncertainty.

 186
